Invariant Gibbs measure for a Schrödinger equation with exponential nonlinearity
Tristan Robert

Abstract
. We investigate the invariance of the Gibbs measure for the fractional Schrödinger equation of exponential type (expNLS) i ​ ∂ t u + ( − Δ ) α 2 ​ u = 2 ​ γ ​ β ​ e β ​ | u | 2 ​ u i\partial_{t}u+(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u=2\gamma\beta e^{\beta|u|^{2}}u on d d -dimensional compact Riemannian manifolds ℳ d \mathcal{M}_{d} , for a dispersion parameter α > d \alpha>d , some coupling constant β > 0 \beta>0 , and γ ≠ 0 \gamma\neq 0 . (i) We first study the construction of the Gibbs measure for (expNLS). We prove that in the defocusing case γ > 0 \gamma>0 , the measure is well-defined in the whole regime α > d \alpha>d and β > 0 \beta>0 (Theorem 1.1 (i)), while in the focusing case γ < 0 \gamma<0 its partition function is always infinite for any α > d \alpha>d and β > 0 \beta>0 , even with a mass cut-off of arbitrary small size (Theorem 1.1 (ii)). (ii) We then study the dynamics (expNLS) with random initial data of low regularity. We first use a compactness argument to prove weak invariance of the Gibbs measure in the whole regime α > d \alpha>d and 0 < β < β α ⋆ 0<\beta<\beta^{\star}_{\alpha} for some natural parameter 0 < β α ⋆ ∼ ( α − d ) 0<\beta^{\star}_{\alpha}\sim(\alpha-d) (Theorem 1.3 (i)). In the large dispersion regime α > 2 ​ d \alpha>2d , we can improve this result by constructing a local deterministic flow for (expNLS) for any β > 0 \beta>0 . Using the Gibbs measure, we prove that solutions are almost surely global for 0 < β ≪ β α ⋆ 0<\beta\ll\beta^{\star}_{\alpha} , and that the Gibbs measure is invariant (Theorem 1.3 (ii)). (iii) Finally, in the particular case d = 1 d=1 and ℳ = 𝕋 \mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T} , we are able to exploit some probabilistic multilinear smoothing effects to build a probabilistic flow for (expNLS) for 1 + 2 2 < α ≤ 2 1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}<\alpha\leq 2 , locally for arbitrary β > 0 \beta>0 and globally for 0 < β ≪ β α ⋆ 0<\beta\ll\beta^{\star}_{\alpha} (Theorem 1.5 ).



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35Q41

1.
Introduction

1.1.
The exponential NLS

The purpose of this work is to investigate the construction and the invariance of the Gibbs measure for a nonlinear fractional Schrödinger equation of exponential type

(1.1)

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}u+(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u=2\gamma\beta e^{\beta|u|^{2}}u,\hskip 14.22636pt(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}_{d},

where
(\mathcal{M}_{d},\mathbf{\mathrm{g}})
is a closed (compact, boundaryless) Riemannian manifold of dimension
d\geq 1
,
\alpha>0
is the dispersion parameter,
\beta>0
is the coupling constant, and
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
,
\gamma\neq 0
encodes the nature of the nonlinear interaction (defocusing for
\gamma>0
, focusing when
\gamma<0
).

The model (
1.1
) appears naturally in the context of nonlinear optics
[
40
]
. From a mathematical perspective, the exponential nonlinearity was proposed as the natural energy critical nonlinearity for the Schrödinger equation in dimension
d=2
. Indeed, recall that in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the pure power nonlinear fractional Schrödinger equation

(1.2)

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}u+(-\Delta)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u=\pm|u|^{p-1}u

has scaling critical regularity

(1.3)

\displaystyle s_{c}(p,\alpha)=\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\alpha}{p-1}.

In particular, rewriting (
1.1
) as

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}u+(-\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u=2\gamma\beta\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\beta^{k}}{k!}|u|^{2k}u,

we see from (
1.3
) that for (
1.1
) the scaling critical regularity is formally

(1.4)

\displaystyle s_{c}(\infty,\alpha)=\frac{d}{2}

for any
\alpha
. The energy (or Hamiltonian) of (
1.1
) being given by

(1.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\beta}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{M}_{d}}\big|\langle\nabla\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u\big|^{2}dx+\gamma\int_{\mathcal{M}_{d}}e^{\beta|u|^{2}}dx,

we thus see that
d=\alpha=2
makes (
1.1
) energy critical. This motivated the study of (
1.1
) for
\alpha=2
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}

[
19
,
47
,
20
,
46
,
38
,
66
,
67
]
. In particular, building on the previous global well-posedness result for small data for the
1
1

1

In
[
20
]
, the Schrödinger equation with nonlinearity
e^{4\pi|u|^{2}}u-u
is considered, which is equivalent to (
1.1
) via a gauge transform. In
[
38
]
the nonlinearity
e^{4\pi|u|^{2}}u-u-4\pi|u|^{2}u
is treated.
defocusing (expNLS) of
[
20
]
, Ibrahim, Majdoub, Masmoudi, and Nakanishi proved in
[
38
]
that scattering occurs for the small data global solutions of (
1.1
) when
\alpha=d=2
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and with
\beta=4\pi
related to the sharp constant in Moser-Trudinger’s inequality
[
62
,
44
]
.

In the case of a compact domain, however, scattering cannot occur, so that one can ask what the long-time behaviour of the flow looks like. The pioneering works
[
41
,
8
,
9
]
on the standard NLS (
1.2
) with
\alpha=2
on
\mathbb{T}^{d}
,
d=1,2
, paved the way to a probabilistic approach to this question. Namely, in view of Poincaré’s recurrence theorem, one can look for the construction of a probability measure invariant under the flow of (
1.1
), which then indicates that the flow is recurrent, at least at the level of regularity of the support of the invariant measure. The probabilistic approach to nonlinear dispersive PDEs has since then attracted a tremendous amount of interest. We refer e.g. to
[
5
,
8
,
9
,
14
,
16
,
17
,
21
,
41
,
45
,
57
,
58
,
60
,
63
,
64
,
68
]
and references therein for further examples and discussion on the construction and invariance of Gibbs measure for NLS type equations. In the following, we study this approach for the model (
1.1
).

1.2.
Gibbs measure associated to the Hamiltonian

The exponential NLS equation (
1.1
) is Hamiltonian, with Hamiltonian given by
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\beta}
(
1.5
). In particular
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\beta}
is conserved along the flow, and so is the mass

(1.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{M}_{d}}|u|^{2}dx.

In view of the conservation of these two quantities, we expect that any measure given formally by

\displaystyle``F(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\beta})du"

for some function
F:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
will be conserved by the flow of (
1.1
). In particular, we aim at defining the Gibbs type measure

(1.7)

\displaystyle``d\rho_{\alpha,\beta}(u)=\exp\Big(-\mathcal{J}(u)-\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\beta}(u)\Big)du".

The usual way is to write
\mathcal{J}+\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\beta}
as a quadratic part in
u
(the kinetic energy) plus a potential term

(1.8)

\displaystyle V_{\beta}(u)=\int_{\mathcal{M}_{d}}e^{\beta|u|^{2}}dx,

and then to interpret

\displaystyle d\rho_{\alpha,\beta}(u)=\exp(-\gamma V_{\beta}(u))\times\exp\Big\{-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{M}_{d}}\big|\langle\nabla\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u\big|^{2}dx\Big\}du,

where
\langle\cdot\rangle=\sqrt{1+|\cdot|^{2}}
.

The last part

(1.9)

\displaystyle``d\mu_{\alpha}(u)=\exp\Big\{-\int_{\mathcal{M}_{d}}\big|\langle\nabla\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u\big|^{2}dx\Big\}du"

can be interpreted as a Gaussian measure on
L^{2}(\mathcal{M}_{d})
. Indeed, expanding
u
on an eigenbasis
\{\varphi_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
of
L^{2}(\mathcal{M}_{d})
associated with the eigenvalues
\{-\lambda_{n}^{2}\}_{n\geq 0}
of the Laplace-Beltrami operator
\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}}
on
(\mathcal{M}_{d},\mathbf{\mathrm{g}})
, we can formally write

\displaystyle d\mu_{\alpha}(u)=\exp\Big\{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\alpha}|\widehat{u}_{n}|^{2}\Big\}\Big(\prod_{n\geq 0}d\widehat{u}_{n}\Big)=\prod_{n\geq 0}e^{-\frac{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\alpha}|\widehat{u}_{n}|^{2}}{2}}d\widehat{u}_{n},

where
\widehat{u}_{n}
stands for the
n
-th Fourier coefficient of
u\in L^{2}(\mathcal{M}_{d})
,
n\geq 0
, i.e.
\widehat{u}_{n}=\langle u,\varphi_{n}\rangle
where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
stands for the usual inner product in
L^{2}(\mathcal{M}_{d})
. In particular, up to a constant factor we can recognize
e^{-\frac{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\alpha}|\widehat{u}_{n}|^{2}}{2}}d\widehat{u}_{n}
as a standard centered (complex-valued) Gaussian measure with variance
\frac{1}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\alpha}}
. This allows us to define properly the measure
\mu_{\alpha}
as the law of the random series

(1.10)

\displaystyle u_{\alpha}^{\omega}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{g_{n}(\omega)}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\varphi_{n},

where
\{g_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
is a family of independent standard complex-valued Gaussian random variables on some probability space
(\Omega,\mathbb{P})
. Here the convergence of the series in (
1.10
) holds in
L^{2}(\Omega;H^{s}(\mathcal{M}_{d}))
if and only if

(1.11)

\displaystyle s<\frac{\alpha-d}{2};

see Lemma
2.4
below.

In view of the previous discussion, our first result is concerned with the rigorous definition of the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
in (
1.7
). For
N\geq 1
, we first define the truncated version

(1.12)

\displaystyle d\rho_{\alpha,\beta,N}(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha,\beta,N}^{-1}e^{-\gamma V_{\beta}(\Pi_{\leq N}u)}d\mu_{\alpha}(u),

where
\Pi_{\leq N}
is as above, and
\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha,\beta,N}=\int e^{-\gamma V_{\beta}(\Pi_{\leq N}u)}d\mu_{\alpha}(u)
is the partition function of
\rho_{\alpha,\beta,N}
.

Theorem 1.1
.

The following hold for any
d\geq 1
,
\alpha>d
and
\beta>0
:

(i) (Defocusing case)
If
\gamma>0
, then
{\displaystyle e^{-\gamma V_{\beta}(u)}\in L^{1}(\mu_{\alpha})}
. In particular the Gibbs measure

(1.13)

\displaystyle d\rho_{\alpha,\beta}(u)=\frac{e^{-\gamma V_{\beta}(u)}}{\int e^{-\gamma V_{\beta}(u)}d\mu_{\alpha}(u)}d\mu_{\alpha}(u)

is a well-defined probability measure on
H^{s}(\mathcal{M}_{d})
,
s<\frac{\alpha-d}{2}
, which is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu_{\alpha}
. Moreover,
\rho_{\alpha,\beta,N}\to\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
in total variation, as
N\to\infty
.

(ii) (Focusing case)
If
\gamma<0
, then for any
K>0
,
{\displaystyle e^{-\gamma V_{\beta}(u)}\mathbf{1}_{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K}\not\in L^{1}(\mu_{\alpha})}
. In particular the Gibbs measure, even with an arbitrarily small mass cut-off, cannot be defined as a probability measure.

Let us comment on Theorem
1.1
. In the non-singular regime
\alpha>d
which Theorem
1.1
is concerned with, the potential
V_{\beta}
(
1.8
) is almost surely well-defined on the support of the Gaussian measure
\mu_{\alpha}
(
1.9
) (see Lemma
2.4
below). Since it is bounded below, the construction of the Gibbs measure in the defocusing case in Theorem
1.1
(i) is rather straightforward; see Subsection
2.2
. In comparison, in the singular case
\alpha=d
and for polynomial interactions, much effort has been made in constructive quantum field theory
[
29
,
55
]
to construct the Gibbs measure. Indeed in this latter case the potential needs to be renormalized through an approximation procedure and Wick ordering, which in particular makes it non bounded below. We refer to
[
54
]
for a pedestrian construction in the case
\alpha=d=2
and polynomial interaction in place of
V_{\beta}
.

The focusing case of Theorem
1.1
(ii) is more subtle. In the seminal paper
[
41
]
, Lebowitz, Rose, and Speer studied the construction of the Gibbs measure for the standard (NLS) (
1.2
) with
\alpha=2
in dimension
d=1
on
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{T}
for
p\leq 6
, and showed that the measure is finite for arbitrary mass cut-off in the case
p<5
, and in the case
p=6
for small enough mass cut-off depending on the size of the ground state associated to (
1.2
). The same result as Theorem
1.1
(ii) holds in particular for (
1.2
) with
d=1
,
\alpha=2
and
p>6
. See also
[
61
]
for a simple proof of this result. Since
V_{\beta}(u)\geq\frac{\beta^{k}}{k!}|u|^{2k}
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the case
\alpha=2
,
d=1
of Theorem
1.1
(ii) is thus straightforward from
[
41
,
61
]
, at least for large
K
. Similarly,
[
14
]
proves that the partition function for the focusing Gibbs measure associated with the quartic potential
|u|^{4}
in place of
V_{\beta}
in the case
\alpha=d=2
is infinite. However, one could have hoped that taking
\alpha>d
large enough and
K,\beta>0
small enough might allow the construction of the mass-truncated focusing Gibbs measure. Theorem
1.1
(ii) shows that this is not the case. In the case of large
K
, a simple argument similar to that of
[
41
]
, based on Cameron-Martin’s theorem and a choice of deterministic drift
2
2

2

Here we use properly scaled functions supported at high frequency instead of concentrating solitons as in
[
41
]
. See Lemma
2.6
below.
with energy blowing-up to
-\infty
suffices to prove Theorem
1.1
(ii) for any
\alpha>d
and any
\beta>0
: see e.g. Proposition A.1 in
[
51
]
in the context of an exponential interaction. To show that Theorem
1.1
(ii) also holds for arbitrarily small
K
, we use a more subtle construction introduced in
[
48
]
and refined in
[
53
]
, based on a variational formulation to express the partition function of the Gibbs measure; see
[
6
,
65
]
and (
2.7
) below. This latter formulation proved to be an effective tool in the construction of singular Gibbs measure with polynomial interactions in the defocusing case mentioned above
[
3
,
48
,
13
,
49
]
. It allows to replace the deterministic drift in Cameron-Martin’s formula with one built on the random variable
u_{\alpha}
(
1.10
) with law
\mu_{\alpha}
. Taking formally
-u_{\alpha}
plus a perturbation with large negative energy as a drift then allows to get the result.

Remark 1.2
.

(i) In the critical case
\alpha=d
, the measure
\mu_{\alpha}
becomes supported on distributions rather than functions. This can be easily seen by computing for any
N\geq 0
and
x\in\mathcal{M}_{d}
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha,N}(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\big|\Pi_{\leq N}u_{\alpha}^{\omega}(x)\big|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\Big|\sum_{\lambda_{n}\leq N}\frac{g_{n}}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\varphi_{n}(x)\Big|^{2}

(1.14)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda_{n}\leq N}\frac{|\varphi_{n}(x)|^{2}}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\alpha}}\sim\sum_{\lambda_{n}\leq N}\frac{1}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\alpha}},

uniformly in
N\geq 1
, where
\Pi_{\leq N}
stands for the spectral projector on the set of frequencies
\{\lambda_{n}\leq N\}
, and where the last estimate follows from Corollary
2.2
below. From Weyl’s law

(1.15)

\displaystyle\lambda_{n}\sim n^{\frac{1}{d}}

we get that the partial sums
\sigma_{\alpha,N}
in (
1.2
) then converges in
L^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
to

(1.16)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\alpha}(x)=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{|\varphi_{n}(x)|^{2}}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\alpha}}

if and only if
\alpha>d
.

As a result, any nonlinear potential (and in particular
V_{\beta}
) would need a renormalization to be able to define the Gibbs measure. As mentioned above, in the defocusing case, for
\alpha=d=2
and a polynomial interaction, this is done in
[
54
]
. For the same regime and replacing the Gaussian measure
\mu_{\alpha}
by the law
\mu_{\alpha}^{\mathbb{R}}
of
\Re(u_{\alpha})
, the defocusing Gibbs measure with (Wick renormalized) exponential interaction
e^{\beta u}
in place of
V_{\beta}
was proved to be constructible if and only if
\beta^{2}<8\pi
in
[
34
]
; see also
[
24
,
25
,
31
,
36
,
37
,
52
,
51
]
for recent results on this model, in particular the invariance of the Gibbs measure for the stochastic heat and wave dynamics. The point is that when
\alpha=d
, the random variable
u_{\alpha}
(or
\Re(u_{\alpha})
) is log-correlated, namely
G(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}[u_{\alpha}(x)\overline{u}_{\alpha}(y)]\sim\frac{1}{2\pi}\log d(x,y)
. By property of the Wick renormalization, the renormalized exponential
\,:\!e^{\beta u}\!:\,
has then correlation function
\exp\big(\beta G(x,y)\big)\sim d(x,y)^{\frac{\beta^{2}}{4\pi}}
which is locally integrable if and only if
\beta^{2}<8\pi
. Note however that there is no Schrödinger analogue of the dynamics studied in
[
36
,
37
,
52
,
51
]
for the potential
e^{\beta u}
, since this latter does not generate a Hamiltonian dynamics for NLS.

(ii) In order to get existence of the measure
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
(
1.7
) for some
\beta>0
in the critical case
\alpha=d
, a renormalization procedure would thus also be needed. This amounts to defining the renormalized truncated potential for
N\geq 1
as

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{\beta,N}(u)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\beta^{k}}{k!}\,:\!|\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2k}\!:\,dx

(1.17)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\frac{1}{1+\beta\sigma_{\alpha,N}(x)}\exp\Big(\frac{\beta}{1+\beta\sigma_{\alpha,N}(x)}|\Pi_{\leq N}u(x)|^{2}\Big)dx.

Here
\,:\!|\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2k}\!:\,=(-1)^{k}(k!)L_{k}\big(|\Pi_{\leq N}u(x)|^{2};\sigma_{N}(x)\big)
denotes the Wick renormalization of the potential
|u|^{2k}
, where
L_{k}(y;\sigma)
are the generalized Laguerre polynomials. The last formula then follows from the expression for the generating function of the
L_{k}
, see e.g.
[
54
]
. Then one should prove convergence in measure of
\mathbb{V}_{\beta,N}
for some
\beta>0
. One major difficulty here is that one deals with the exponential of
|u_{\alpha}|^{2}
instead of
\Re(u_{\alpha})
as discussed above, which is not Gaussian anymore, and with correlation function behaving like
\big(\log d(x,y)\big)^{2}
. Thus it is not clear at the moment whether the Gibbs measure with renormalized potential (
1.2
) is well-defined, even in the defocusing case. We plan to pursue this construction in a follow-up work.

(iii) On the other hand, for
\alpha=d
, Oh, Seong, and Tolomeo showed in
[
53
]
that the Gibbs measure has again infinite mass in the focusing case for the renormalized quartic potential
\,:\!|u|^{4}\!:\,
in place of
V_{\beta}(u)
, even with a (Wick-ordered) mass cut-off of arbitrarily small size. Thus in the focusing case, we expect that the Gibbs measure associated with the renormalized potential (
1.2
) is also ill-defined.

1.3.
Invariance of the Gibbs measure

We now move on to the construction of solutions to (
1.1
) leaving
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
invariant.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
d\geq 1
and
\alpha>d
. Then the following hold:

(i) (Weak invariance for subcritical dispersion)
For any
0<\beta<\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}
and
0<s<\frac{\alpha-d}{2}
, there is a random variable
u
in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathcal{M}_{d}))
which solves (
1.1
) in the distributional sense. Moreover,
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
is invariant under the map
u(0)\mapsto u(t)
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

(ii) (Strong invariance for higher dispersion)
If
\alpha>2d
, for any
0<\beta\ll\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}
, the exponential NLS (
1.1
) on
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{\mathrm{g}})
is
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
-almost surely globally well-posed, and the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
is invariant under the flow. More precisely: for
\frac{d}{2}<s<\frac{\alpha-d}{2}
, any
\beta>0
and
u_{0}\in H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
, there exists
T>0
and a unique solution to the Cauchy problem for (
1.1
) with data
u_{0}
in
C([-T,T];H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
. Moreover, if
\Phi(t):u_{0}\mapsto u(t)
denotes the local flow map of (
1.1
) on
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
, in the case
0<\beta\ll\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}
there exists
\Sigma\subset H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
of full
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
-measure such that for any
u_{0}\in\Sigma
,
\Phi(t,u_{0})
is globally defined, and
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
is invariant under
\Phi(t)
, in the sense that for any
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
-measurable
A\subset\Sigma
it holds
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}\big(\Phi(t)(A)\big)=\rho_{\alpha,\beta}(A)
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

The first part of Theorem
1.3
deals with the full sub-critical regime
\alpha>d
, but we can only claim existence of weak solutions to (
1.1
) having
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
as an invariant measure, but not the construction of a flow (in particular, no uniqueness) on the support of
\mu_{\alpha}
. On the other hand, Theorem
1.3
(ii) is concerned with strong invariance of the Gibbs measure, in the sense that the equation (
1.1
) is (unconditionally) globally well-posed on the support of
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
, and in particular there is uniqueness of the solution. Theorem
1.3
(ii) then improves this latter result to get the uniqueness of the weak solutions constructed in Theorem
1.3
(i). In the general setting of the above theorem we can however only deal with the high dispersion regime
\alpha>2d
.

Theorem
1.3
(i) is proved using a very flexible compactness argument first introduced in
[
1
,
22
]
and then developed in the context of dispersive PDEs in
[
17
]
. This is based on the study of the approximate dynamics

(1.18)

\displaystyle(i\partial_{t}+(-\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}})u_{N}=2\gamma\beta\Pi_{\leq N}\Big[e^{\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2}}\Pi_{\leq N}u\Big].

Since
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\rho_{\alpha,\beta,N}
, it is natural to look at the Hamiltonian dynamics (
1.18
) which leaves
\rho_{\alpha,\beta,N}
invariant. Then one proves that there is a subsequence
N_{k}\to\infty
and a sequence
\widetilde{u}_{N_{k}}
of
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
-valued random variables with the same law
\rho_{\alpha,\beta,N}
as
u_{N_{k}}
such that
\widetilde{u}_{N_{k}}
converges almost surely in
C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
to some random variable
u
, which is proved to be a distributional solution to (
1.1
). The convergence
\rho_{\alpha,\beta,N}\to\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
then ensures that
u
has
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
has an invariant measure.

For Theorem
1.3
(ii), the dispersion
\alpha>2d
is large enough so that the support of the Gibbs measure lies at sub-critical regularity in view of (
1.4
) and (
1.11
). Thus local well-posedness can be proved in a purely deterministic manner. However, note that the support
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
,
s<\frac{\alpha-d}{2}
of
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
and
\mu_{\alpha}
(note that
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}\ll\mu_{\alpha}
) is always larger than the energy space
H^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}(\mathcal{M})
, which makes global well-posedness for deterministic initial data non trivial since there are no conservation laws at this level of regularity. Here we rely on Bourgain’s invariant measure argument
[
8
]
, which exploits the invariance of the Gibbs measure as a replacement for a conservation law at low regularity, to get almost sure globalization of the local flow map. This argument allows us to construct a set of large
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
-measure for which we can iterate a great number of times the deterministic local Cauchy theory without the solution to grow too much. The restriction on
0<\beta\ll\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}
thus comes from the smallness of the local time compared to the
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
-measure of balls of
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
(see the tail estimate in Lemma
2.4
below).

Remark 1.4
.

In
[
10
,
11
,
12
]
, Bourgain and Bulut developped a flexible argument leading to a stronger result than that provided by the compactness method. Namely, their method provides convergence of the whole sequence
u_{N}
for the truncated dynamics and without changing the base probability space as in the compactness method. However this usually does not provide us with a flow map. See the discussion in
[
57
]
. In particular, in
[
57
, Theorem 7]
, Sun and Tzvetkov proved the convergence of the solutions
u_{N}
to the truncated dynamics associated with the defocusing fractional NLS (
1.2
), in the whole range
\alpha>d
, and for any
p>1
. This argument relies on the observation that the nonlinear flow has, with high probability, the same enhanced integrability property than that of the linear flow (see (
1.21
) below). This allows to obtain, with high probability, good controls of the truncated dynamics
u_{N}
for times
\tau_{N}=O\big((\log N)^{-\frac{1}{p}}\big)
, related to the local time of existence in the Cauchy theory. However in our case (
1.18
) we have formally
p\to\infty
, and thus we should replace
\tau_{N}
by
e^{-c(\log N)^{2}}
. This makes the number of iterations needed to control
u_{N}
on times
O(1)
too large so that we cannot ensure the summability of the probabilities to have bad controls for each
N
, even along dyadic sequences. So it seems that this argument fails in our context.

When
\mathcal{M}_{d}=\mathbb{T}
, we can improve on the general result of Theorem
1.3
by using probabilistic multilinear smoothing effects to get existence and uniqueness of solutions for some
\alpha\leq 2d
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
d=1
and
\mathcal{M}_{d}=\mathbb{T}
with the standard metric. For any
1+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}<\alpha\leq 2
, the following hold:

(i) (Almost sure local well-posedness)
For any
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta>0
, the Cauchy problem for (
1.1
) is
\mu_{\alpha}
-almost surely locally well-posed. More precisely: for
0<s<\frac{\alpha-1}{2}
, there exists
\Sigma\subset H^{s}(\mathbb{T})
of full
\mu_{\alpha}
-measure such that for any
u_{0}\in\Sigma
, there exists
T>0
and a solution to the Cauchy problem for (
1.1
) on
[-T,T]
with data
u_{0}
, unique in the class

(1.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}^{-1}\big(e^{it(-\partial_{x}^{2})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}u_{0}+X^{s_{1},b}(T)\big),

for some
s_{1},b>\frac{1}{2}
.

(ii) (Almost sure global well-posedness and invariance of the Gibbs measure)
If
\gamma>0
and
0<\beta\ll\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}
, then the Cauchy problem for (
1.1
) is
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
-almost surely globally well-posed, and the Gibbs measure
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
is invariant under the flow. More precisely, if
\Sigma
is as in
(i)
, and if
\Phi(t):u_{0}\mapsto u(t)
denotes the flow map of (
1.1
) on
\Sigma
, then
\Phi(\Sigma)=\Sigma
, and
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
is invariant under
\Phi(t)
in the sense that for any
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
-measurable
A\subset\Sigma
it holds
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}\big(\Phi(t)(A)\big)=\rho_{\alpha,\beta}(A)
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Here the gauge transform
\mathbb{G}
and the Bourgain space
X^{s_{1},b}(T)
are defined in Subsection
5.2
below.

When
\alpha\leq 2d
, the support of the Gibbs measure (
1.11
) then lies in spaces of super-critical regularity, and thus a purely deterministic approach to the Cauchy problem for (
1.1
) on the support of
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
fails, so that the solutions obtained in Theorem
1.5
are a manifestation of a probabilistic smoothing effect.

The proof of Theorem
1.5
relies on the now classical probabilistic approach of Bourgain
[
9
]
and consists at looking for a solution to (
1.1
) under the form

On the one hand, the randomization provides the linear evolution
\mathchoice{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to4.1pt{\vbox to7.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-0.28453pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}{{}}{}

starting from
\widetilde{w}(0)=0
. Thus we need to control uniformly all the multilinear forms in the right-hand side of (
1.22
), at spatial regularity
s_{1}>\frac{1}{2}
. The most problematic terms are the high-low interactions involving a high frequency piece of the random linear evolution
since this latter only has regularity
\frac{\alpha-1}{2}-<\frac{1}{2}
for
\alpha\leq 2
. To be able to systematically exploit some multilinear smoothing effect uniformly in all the multilinear forms, we make a gauge transform

\displaystyle v(t)=\exp\Big\{-i\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{G}\big(|u(t^{\prime})|\big)dt^{\prime}\Big\}u(t)

for some choice of gauge
\mathcal{G}
; see (
5.18
) below. In particular, note that this gauge transform does not affect the initial data. Then the new remainder

The point now is that in the expansion (
1.3
), some resonances have been removed in the
(2k+1)
-linear forms
\mathcal{Q}_{2k+1}
compared to the original power nonlinearities
|u|^{2k}u
. This allows us to get some nonlinear smoothing effect and to run a fixed point argument in
X^{s_{1},b}
to construct
w
. This provides local well-posedness of the remainder
w
in (
1.3
), and thus of the solution
u
to (
1.1
) after inverting the gauge transform

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}+w
. The global well-posedness and the invariance of the Gibbs measure are then established through Bourgain’s invariant measure argument
[
8
,
9
]
. Here, compared to the implementation of this argument for Theorem
1.3
(ii), it is more involved due to the space of initial data
Y^{s}(\mathbb{T})\not\subset H^{s_{1}}(\mathbb{T})
used to build the probabilistic local flow; see the definition of
Y^{s}(\mathbb{T})
in (
5.8
) below. In order to iterate the local probabilistic Cauchy theory, we thus need to control the flow on the sum space
Y^{s}(\mathbb{T})+H^{s_{1}}(\mathbb{T})
as in
[
57
]
.

Remark 1.6
.

Observe that even the softest argument of Theorem
1.3
(i) requires
0<\beta<\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}\sim\alpha-d\longrightarrow 0
as
\alpha\searrow d
. This prevents us for example from proving that the family of measures
\{\rho_{\alpha,\beta}\}_{\alpha}
is tight for some fixed
\beta>0
in order to try and treat the critical case
\alpha=d
. It is not clear if this issue persists after renormalizing the potential as in Remark
1.2
(ii).

Remark 1.7

(Exponential interaction in the wave case)
.

In
[
56
]
, Sun and Tzvetkov considered the invariance of Gibbs measure for the fractional wave equation with exponential nonlinearity

(1.24)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}+(-\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}+1)u+\beta e^{\beta u}=0

in the whole range
\alpha>d
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Writing the Duhamel formulation

\displaystyle u(t)=\cos(t\langle\nabla\rangle^{\alpha})u(0)+\frac{\sin(t\langle\nabla\rangle^{\alpha})}{\langle\nabla\rangle^{\alpha}}\partial_{t}u(0)+\beta\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\sin\big((t-t^{\prime})\langle\nabla\rangle^{\alpha}\big)}{\langle\nabla\rangle^{\alpha}}e^{\beta u(t^{\prime})}dt^{\prime},

we see that the smoothing property of the propagator for the wave equation (i.e. the smoothing term
\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\alpha}
in the Duhamel formula) allows for a direct gain of smoothness and thus there is no need for exploiting multilinear smoothing in this case.

In
[
52
]
, thanks to this gain of smoothness, the author with Oh and Wang was able to treat (
1.24
) in the case
\alpha=d=2
and some small
|\beta|
. The argument relied on a refinement of the linear ansatz (
1.20
), in the spirit of the theory of para-controlled distributions used in the setting of singular parabolic stochastic equations
[
30
]
, but did not exploit any dispersive multilinear smoothing effect either.

Remark 1.8

(Invariant Gibbs measure for the cubic fractional NLS)
.

In
[
57
]
, Sun and Tzvetkov considered the invariance of Gibbs measure for the cubic fractional NLS (
1.2
) for
d=1
,
p=3
and some
\alpha<2
. They used the same ansatz (
1.20
) to build a probabilistic flow map as in Theorem
1.5
, but in their case the multilinear estimates required to close the fixed point argument in
X^{s_{1},b}
for the remainder
w
are more involved than the one used for Theorem
1.5
. Indeed in our case the sub-critical space in which we build
w
contains
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{T})
, which allows for more straightforward multilinear estimates. They also established analogue of Theorem
1.3
(i) even for some
\alpha<1
, and proved a stronger convergence statement in the case
\alpha>1
; see Remark
1.4
above. Our study is thus similar in spirit to that of
[
57
]
, namely using
\alpha
as a way to test the roughness of initial data allowed in each of the methods describes above. In our case we also use the coupling constant
\beta>0
as a way to quantify the moment bounds with respect to
\mu_{\alpha}
needed in the arguments. Indeed, a major difference between the polynomial and the exponential cases is that in the former case, the various nonlinear expressions of the random initial data belong to Wiener chaoses of finite degree, from which exponential tail estimates follow directly thanks to the Wiener chaos estimate. In our case, even the integrability of
V_{\beta}(u)
requires limitation on the range of
\beta>0
. See Lemma
3.1
below.

Remark 1.9

(Probabilistic scaling criticality and random averaging operators/random tensors)
.

It might be possible to refine the argument below for the multilinear estimates involved in the proof of Theorem
1.5
in the case of High
\times
High
\to
High interactions to show that we can take
\alpha>\frac{3}{2}
in Theorem
1.5
, and that this threshold for the dispersion solely comes from the control on High
\times
Low
\to
High interactions with a linear evolution of the random initial data as a high regularity input. This is precisely the bad interaction that is amenable to the more sophisticated method of random averaging operators and random tensors developed recently in
[
26
,
27
]
. Thus we believe that there is a chance that Theorem
1.5
can be improved all the way down to the range
\alpha>1
. See also the discussions in a similar context in
[
57
,
58
]
. However, note that in the critical case
\alpha=d
, the exponential NLS appears to be probabilistic critical in the sense of
[
26
,
27
]
, and it is not clear at all that the methods developed in these papers could handle this case due to combinatorics losses. Namely, after decomposing the (renormalized) nonlinearity in (
1.2
) as

{\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\big(\frac{-\beta}{1-\beta\sigma_{\alpha}}\big)^{k}\mathcal{N}_{2k+1}(v)}
where the
(2k+1)
-linear forms
\mathcal{N}_{2k+1}
are simple (see
[
26
,
27
]
), it seems that the estimates in
[
26
]
for
\alpha=d=2
are of the form

\|\mathcal{N}_{2k+1}(v)\|_{X^{s,b-1}}\leq C^{k}(k!)\|v\|_{X^{s,b}}^{2k+1}

which is not summable.

Acknowledgements
.

The author is very thankful to Laurent Thomann for pointing out this problem and for interesting discussions. Part of this work was supported by the German Research Foundation (DFG) through the CRC1283 “Taming uncertainty and profiting from randomness and low regularity in analysis, stochastics and their applications”.

2.
Study of the Gibbs measure

In this section, we introduce the basic tools needed for the proofs of Theorems
1.1
,
1.3
and
1.5
. We start with recalling some basic tools from analysis on manifolds in Subsection
2.1
, and establish some standard large deviation estimates for
\mu_{\alpha}
as well as the proof of Theorem
1.1
(i) in Subsection
2.2
. Subsections
2.3
,
2.4
and
2.5
are then devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.1
(ii).

2.1.
Background on analysis on manifolds

Let
(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{\mathrm{g}})
be a closed (compact, boundaryless) smooth Riemannian manifold of dimension
d\geq 1
. In local coordinates, the metric
\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}
is given by a smooth function
x\mapsto(\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}_{j,k}(x))_{j,k}
taking value in the set of positive symmetric definite matrices. In particular
(\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}_{j,k})
is invertible and its inverse is denoted by

\displaystyle\big(\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}^{j,k}(x)\big)=\big(\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}_{j,k}(x)\big)^{-1}.

We also write

\displaystyle|\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}(x)|=\det\big(\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}_{j,k}(x)\big)>0.

The volume form
\mathrm{Vol}
can then be written locally as

\displaystyle d\mathrm{Vol}(x)=|\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}(x)|^{\frac{1}{2}}dx,

and abusing notations we will write
dx
in place of
d\mathrm{Vol}
.

The Laplace-Beltrami operator is given in local coordinates by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}}f=|\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}|^{-\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{j}(|\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}^{j,k}\partial_{k}f\big)

for any smooth function
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
. Here summation on repeated indices is implicit.

It is well-known (see e.g.
[
39
, Chapter 2]
) that there exists a basis
\{\varphi_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}\subset C^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})
of
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
consisting of eigenfunctions of
\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}}
associated with the eigenvalue
-\lambda_{n}^{2}
, assumed to be arranged in increasing order:
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq...
and satisfying
\lambda_{n}\to\infty
. Moreover, any
f\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M})
can be expanded (in the sense of distributions) as

\displaystyle f=\sum_{n\geq 0}\langle f,\varphi_{n}\rangle\varphi_{n},

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the usual duality pairing between
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M})
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
which coincides with the
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
-inner product when
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
. This allows to define, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
, the Sobolev spaces

\displaystyle W^{s,p}(\mathcal{M},\mathbf{\mathrm{g}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}\Big\{u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}),~\|u\|_{W^{s,p}(\mathcal{M})}<\infty\Big\}

where

\displaystyle\|u\|_{W^{s,p}(\mathcal{M})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}\big\|(1-\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}})^{\frac{s}{2}}u\big\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{M})}=\Big\|\sum_{n\geq 0}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{s}\langle u,\varphi_{n}\rangle_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}}\varphi_{n}\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mathcal{M})}.

When
p=2
we write
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}W^{s,2}(\mathcal{M})
.

To get some information on the localization of the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}
and bounds on the eigenfunctions
\varphi_{n}
, it is usual to consider the spectral function, which is defined as

(2.1)

\displaystyle e(x,y,\Lambda^{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{\lambda_{n}^{2}\leq\Lambda^{2}}\varphi_{n}(x)\varphi(y),

for
(x,y)\in\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M}
and
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. We have the following behaviour of the spectral function on the diagonal (see
[
35
, Theorem 1.1]
; note that here the
\lambda_{n}
’s are the square roots of the eigenvalues of
-\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}}
).

Lemma 2.1
.

The following asymptotics hold uniformly in
x\in\mathcal{M}
as
\Lambda\to\infty
:

(2.2)

e(x,x,\Lambda^{2})=c_{d}\Lambda^{d}+O(\Lambda^{d-1}),

for some constant
c_{d}>0
only depending on the dimension.

This implies in particular Weyl’s law (
1.15
) after integrating (
2.2
). As a direct consequence we obtain the boundedness in average of the eigenfunctions
\varphi_{n}
.

Corollary 2.2
.

For any
A\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
C>0
such that for any
\Lambda>0
and any
x\in\mathcal{M}
, it holds

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda_{n}\in(\Lambda,\Lambda+1]}\frac{|\varphi_{n}(x)|^{2}}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{A}}\leq C\sum_{\lambda_{n}\in(\Lambda,\Lambda+1]}\frac{1}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{A}}.

Proof.

We just have to use Lemma
2.1
to bound

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda_{n}\in(\Lambda,\Lambda+1]}\frac{\varphi_{n}(x)^{2}}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{A}}

\displaystyle\sim\langle\Lambda\rangle^{-A}\Big[e\big(x,x,(\Lambda+1)^{2}\big)-e(x,x,\Lambda^{2})\Big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\langle\Lambda\rangle^{-A}\Lambda\lesssim\langle\Lambda\rangle^{-A}\#\big\{k:\Lambda<\lambda_{k}\leq\Lambda+1\big\}\sim\sum_{\lambda_{n}\in(\Lambda,\Lambda+1]}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-A}.

This proves Corollary
2.2
.
∎

2.2.
Properties of the Gaussian and Gibbs measures

In this subsection we state the basic properties of the Gaussian measure
\mu_{\alpha}
(
1.9
), which allow us to prove Theorem
1.1
(i).

We start by recalling Khinchin’s inequality.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
(a_{n})\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
g_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{C}}(0,1)
be standard iid complex Gaussian random variables on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
. Then there exists
C>0
such that for any
p\geq 2
it holds

\displaystyle\big\|\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{n}g_{n}\big\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\sqrt{p}\|a_{n}\|_{\ell^{2}}.

Next, we turn to the properties of the random variable
u_{\alpha}
in (
1.10
).

Lemma 2.4
.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, the series defining
u_{\alpha}
in (
1.10
) converges in
L^{p}(\Omega;W^{s,\infty}(\mathcal{M}))
and almost surely in
W^{s,\infty}(\mathcal{M})
, for any
p\geq 1
and
s<\frac{\alpha-d}{2}
. Moreover, for any
1\leq r<+\infty
, there exists
c,C,\delta>0
such that for any any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
R>0
and any
1\leq N_{1}\leq N_{2}\leq\infty
we have the tail estimates

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mu_{\alpha}(\|\Pi_{\leq N_{2}}\Pi_{>N_{1}}u\|_{W^{s,r}}>R)\leq Ce^{-c\|\sigma_{\alpha-2s,N_{2}}-\sigma_{\alpha-2s,N_{1}}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}R^{2}}.

In particular, Lemma
2.4
and the trivial estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}}e^{\beta|u|^{2}}dx\leq\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{M})e^{\beta\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}}

show that for
\alpha>d
, the potential
V_{\beta}(u)
in (
1.8
) is finite
\mu_{\alpha}
-a.s. and
V_{\beta}(\mathbf{P}_{N}u)\to V_{\beta}(u)
a.s. in
C(\mathcal{M})
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
s<\frac{\alpha-d}{2}
, and
\varepsilon>0
such that
s+\varepsilon<\frac{\alpha-d}{2}
. Note that it suffices to prove that the series in (
1.10
) converges in
L^{p}(\Omega;W^{s,\infty}(\mathcal{M}))
for sufficiently large
p
. If
p\geq p_{\varepsilon}>\frac{d}{\varepsilon}
, we have by Sobolev embedding, Minkowki’s inequality, Khinchin’s inequality (Lemma
2.3
) and Corollary
2.2
, that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\|\Pi_{\leq N_{2}}\Pi_{>N_{1}}u_{\alpha}\big\|_{W^{s,\infty}}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}\big\|\Pi_{\leq N_{2}}\Pi_{>N_{1}}u_{\alpha}\big\|_{W^{s+\varepsilon,p_{\varepsilon}}}^{p}\leq C\big\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s+\varepsilon}\Pi_{\leq N_{2}}\Pi_{>N_{1}}u_{\alpha}\big\|_{L^{p_{\varepsilon}}L^{p}_{\omega}}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq C^{p}p^{\frac{p}{2}}\big\|\langle\nabla\rangle^{s+\varepsilon}\Pi_{\leq N_{2}}\Pi_{>N_{1}}u_{\alpha}\big\|_{L^{p_{\varepsilon}}L^{2}_{\omega}}^{p}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x}C^{p}p^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big(\sum_{N_{1}<\lambda_{n}\leq N_{2}}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{2s+2\varepsilon-\alpha}|\varphi_{n}(x)|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{p}{2}}

(2.4)

\displaystyle=C^{p}\|\sigma_{\alpha-2s-2\varepsilon,N_{2}}-\sigma_{\alpha-2s-2\varepsilon,N_{1}}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{p}p^{\frac{p}{2}}.

This proves that
u_{\alpha}\in L^{p}(\Omega;W^{s,\infty}(\mathcal{M}))
. The tail estimate (
2.3
) then follows from Chebychev’s inequality and optimizing (
2.4
) by taking

p\sim\log\big(c\|\sigma_{\alpha-2s-2\varepsilon,N_{2}}-\sigma_{\alpha-2s-2\varepsilon,N_{1}}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}R^{2}\big).

∎

The results above are enough for the construction of the Gibbs measure in the defocusing case.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
(i).

Let us consider the defocusing case
\gamma>0
. Since
\alpha>d
, by Lemma
2.4
,
u_{\alpha}\in W^{s,\infty}(\mathcal{M})
a.s. for any
0\leq s<\frac{\alpha-d}{2}
. In particular
u_{\alpha}\in C(\mathcal{M})
a.s. and so
{\displaystyle V_{\beta}(u_{\alpha})=\int_{\mathcal{M}}e^{\beta|u_{\alpha}|^{2}}dx}
is almost surely finite. Thus
{\displaystyle e^{-\gamma V_{\beta}(u_{\alpha})}}
is almost surely positive, and since
V_{\beta}(u_{\alpha})\geq 0
a.s. and
\gamma>0
it also holds
e^{-\gamma V_{\beta}(u)}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)\subset L^{1}(\Omega)
. This shows that
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
is a well-defined probability measure, absolutely continuous with respect to
\mu_{\alpha}
. As for the almost sure convergence of
V_{\beta}(\Pi_{\leq N}u)
, it follows from the mean value inequality through the estimate

\displaystyle\Big|V_{\beta}(\Pi_{\leq N}u)-V_{\beta}(u)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{M}}\Big|e^{\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2}}-e^{\beta|u|^{2}}\Big|dx\lesssim\int_{\mathcal{M}}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\beta^{k}}{k!}\Big||\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2k}\Pi_{\leq N}u-|u|^{2k}u\Big|dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathcal{M}}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{\beta^{k}}{k!}(2k+1)C^{k}|\Pi_{\leq N}u-u|\big(|\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2k}+|u|^{2k}\big)dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Pi_{\leq N}u-u\|_{L^{2}}e^{\beta C(\|\Pi_{\leq N}u\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2})},

combined with Lemma
2.4
, and that
\|\Pi_{\leq N}u\|_{L^{\infty}}
converges
\mu_{\alpha}
-a.s. and is thus
\mu_{\alpha}
-a.s. finite, uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
. This last claim follows since
\Pi_{\leq N}u(x)
is a martingale in
N\in\mathbb{N}
for all
x\in\mathcal{M}
, and so
\|\Pi_{\leq N}u\|_{L^{\infty}}
is a positive submartingale, uniformly integrable by (
2.4
). Thus it converges
\mu_{\alpha}
-a.s.

The convergence of
\rho_{\alpha,\beta,N}
to
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
in total variation thus follows from the convergence in probability of
V_{\beta}(\Pi_{\leq N}u_{\alpha})
to
V_{\beta}(u_{\alpha})
along with the bound
e^{-\gamma V(\Pi_{\leq N}u)}\leq 1
, uniformly in
N\in\mathbb{N}
. See e.g. the discussion in
[
64
, Remark 3.8]
.
∎

2.3.
Variational formulation

In order to prove Theorem
1.1
(ii),
we rely on a variational formula for the partition function
as in
[
61
,
48
,
53
]
.
Let us first introduce some notations.
Let
W(t)
be a cylindrical Brownian motion in
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
, given by

(2.5)

\displaystyle W(t)=\sum_{n\geq 0}B_{n}(t)\varphi,

where

\{B_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
is a sequence of mutually independent complex-valued
3
3

3

By convention, we normalize
B_{n}
such that
\text{Var}(B_{n}(t))=t
.
Brownian motions.
Then, define a centered Gaussian process
Y(t)

by

(2.6)

\displaystyle Y(t)=\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}W(t).

In particular we have
\mathrm{Law}(Y(1))=\mu_{\alpha}
where
\mu_{\alpha}
is the Gaussian measure (
1.9
).

Next, let
\mathbb{H}_{a}
denote the space of drifts,
which are progressively measurable processes
belonging to

L^{2}([0,1];L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d}))
,
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely. The following Boué-Dupuis formula
[
6
]
is proved in
[
65
, Theorem 7]
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\alpha>d
and
Y
be as in (
2.6
).
For any
0<s<\frac{\alpha-d}{2}
, let
V:W^{s,\infty}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable such that
\mathbb{E}\big[|V(Y(1))|^{p}\big]<\infty

and
\mathbb{E}\big[e^{-qV(Y(1))}\big]<\infty
for some
1<p,q<\infty
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
.
Then, we have

(2.7)

\displaystyle-\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{-V(Y(1))}\Big]=\inf_{\theta\in\mathbb{H}_{a}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[V(Y(1)+I(\theta)(1))+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}dt\bigg],

where
I(\theta)
is defined by

\displaystyle I(\theta)(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\theta(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}

and the expectation
\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}

is an
expectation with respect to the underlying probability measure
\mathbb{P}
.

In the recent works constructing Gibbs measure based on the study of a similar variational formula
[
3
,
32
,
49
]
, a slightly weaker version is used (see e.g. Proposition 4.4 in
[
32
]
) where the expectation in (
2.7
) is replaced by an expectation with respect to a shifted measure depending on the drift. Since in our case we want to construct a drift depending on
Y
(heuristically, we want
I(\theta)(1)=-Y(1)+
perturbation), the formula (
2.7
) is better suited for our purpose.

2.4.
Proof of Theorem
1.1
(ii)

In this subsection, we present the proof of Theorem
1.1
(ii) regarding the non-normalizability of the Gibbs measure
{\displaystyle e^{-\gamma V_{\beta}(u)}\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}d\mu_{\alpha}(u)}
with mass cut-off in the focusing case, for any
\gamma<0
,
\beta>0
,
K>0
and
\alpha>d
.

Note that it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\alpha}}\Big[\exp\big(-\gamma\min(V_{\beta}(u),L)\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}\Big]=+\infty.

Since

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\alpha}}\Big[e^{-\gamma\min(V_{\beta}(u),L)}\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\alpha}}\Big[e^{-\gamma\min(V_{\beta}(u),L)\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}}\Big]-\mu_{\alpha}\big(\|u\|_{L^{2}}>K\big)

(2.8)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\alpha}}\Big[e^{-\gamma\min(V_{\beta}(u),L)\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}}\Big]-1,

the divergence follows once we prove

(2.9)

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\alpha}}\Big[e^{-\gamma\min(V_{\beta}(u),L)\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}}\Big]=+\infty

for any
\alpha>d
,
\beta>0
,
\gamma<0
, and
K>0
.

As for any
L>0
we have
\min(V_{\beta}(u),L)\in L^{\infty}(\mu_{\alpha})
and
\exp\big(-\gamma\min(V_{\beta}(u),L)\big)\in L^{\infty}(\mu_{\alpha})
, we can apply the Boué-Dupuis variational formula
(Lemma
2.5
). Thus we have for any
L>0

\displaystyle-\log\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\alpha}}\Big[e^{-\gamma\min(V_{\beta}(u),L)\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}}\Big]

(2.10)

\displaystyle\qquad=\inf_{\theta\in\mathbb{H}_{a}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\gamma\min\big\{V_{\beta}\big(Y(1)+I(\theta)(1)\big),L\big\}\mathbf{1}_{\{\|Y(1)+I(\theta)(1)\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}dt\bigg],

where
Y(1)
is as in (
2.6
).
Here,
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}
and
\mathbb{E}
denote expectations
with respect to the Gaussian measure
\mu_{\alpha}
in (
1.9
)
and the underlying probability measure
\mathbb{P}
, respectively.
In the following, we show that the right-hand side
of (
2.4
) goes to
-\infty
as
L\to+\infty
.
As mentioned above, the idea is to construct a drift
\theta

such that

I(\theta)\approx-Y(1)+
perturbation. In
[
41
]
, in the case of the focusing nonlinear Schrödinger equation on the circle, corresponding to
d=1
,
\alpha=2
,
\gamma<0
and
V_{\beta}(u)
replaced with the potential
|u|^{p}
,
p>6
, the authors used concentrating solitons as a perturbation having bounded
L^{2}
norm but exploding
L^{p}
norm. Here, as in
[
48
,
53
]
we rely instead on properly scaled functions supported at high frequencies.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\alpha>d
, and fix
x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
. For
N\geq 1
, define the function

(2.11)

\displaystyle f_{N}:x\in\mathcal{M}\mapsto N^{-\frac{d}{2}}\sum_{\lambda_{n}\in(N,2N]}\varphi_{n}(x_{0})\varphi_{n}(x).

Then, there are constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
depending only on
\mathcal{M}
such that for any
N\gg 1
we have

(2.12)

\displaystyle\|f_{N}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=c_{1}+O(N^{-1}),

(2.13)

\displaystyle\|f_{N}\|_{H^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}},

(2.14)

\displaystyle\|f_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{\frac{d}{2}},

and for all
x\in\mathcal{M}
with
d(x,x_{0})\leq c_{2}N^{-1}
, it holds

(2.15)

\displaystyle f_{N}(x)\geq c_{3}N^{\frac{d}{2}}.

We present the proof of Lemma
2.6
in the next subsection. The next lemma, whose proof is also postponed to the next subsection, provides us with an appropriate approximation
Z_{N}
to
Y
in (
2.6
). One should think as
Z_{N}
to be
\Pi_{\leq N}Y
, this latter naive choice being not suitable since
I(\theta)\approx-\Pi_{\leq N}Y(1)
means
\theta\approx-\frac{d}{dt}\Pi_{\leq N}Y\not\in\mathbb{H}_{a}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Given
N\gg 1
, define
Z_{N}=\sum_{\lambda_{n}\leq N}Z_{N,n}\varphi_{n}
, where for
n\geq 0
,
Z_{N,n}
solves the following differential equation
:

(2.16)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}dZ_{N,n}=\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}(\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}B_{n}-Z_{N,n})dt\\
Z_{N,n}|_{t=0}=0,\end{cases}

Then,
Z_{N}(t)
is a centered Gaussian process in
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
which satisfies

(2.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{1}\big\|\frac{d}{dt}Z_{N}(t)\big\|_{H^{s}}^{2}dt\lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{d+2s},

(2.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|Y(1,x)-Z_{N}(1,x)|^{2}\lesssim N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{d-\alpha}\text{ uniformly in }x\in\mathcal{M},

(2.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|Y(1)-Z_{N}(1)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{p}\lesssim p^{\frac{p}{2}}\big(N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{d+2\varepsilon-\alpha}\big)^{\frac{p}{2}}\text{ for }p\gg 1\text{ and }0<\varepsilon<\frac{\alpha-d}{2},

for any
N\gg 1
.

We are now ready to prove the non-normalizability of the focusing Gibbs measure.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
(ii).

In view of the previous discussion, fix
\alpha>d
,
\beta>0
,
\gamma<0
and
K>0
. For
N\gg 1
, let
f_{N}
and
Z_{N}
be given by Lemmas
2.6
and
2.7
, respectively. For the minimization problem (
2.4
),
we take the drift

(2.20)

\displaystyle\theta_{N}(t)=\langle\nabla\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\bigg(-\frac{d}{dt}Z_{N}(t)+\eta f_{N}\bigg)

for some constant
\eta=\eta(K)>0
independent of
N
to be chosen later, and

(2.21)

\displaystyle\Theta_{N}=I(\theta_{N})(1)=\int_{0}^{1}\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\theta(t)\,dt=-Z_{N}(1)+\eta f_{N}.

Then the right-hand side of (
2.4
) is bounded by

(2.22)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\gamma\min\big\{V_{\beta}\big(Y(1)+\Theta_{N}\big),L\big\}\mathbf{1}_{\{\|Y(1)+\Theta_{N}\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta_{N}(t)\|_{L^{2}_{x}}^{2}dt\bigg].

Note that, as in (
2.4
) we have for
L\geq 1

(2.23)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\min\big(V_{\beta}(u),L\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\big\{\min(\beta|u(x)|^{2},\log L)\big\}\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}\Big]dx

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\big\{\min(\beta|u(x)|^{2},\log L)\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}\big\}\Big]dx-\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{M}).

Thus, using (
2.23
) and Jensen’s inequality (with
\gamma<0
), we can bound

(
2.22
)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\int_{\mathcal{M}}\exp\Big[\mathbb{E}\big\{\min(\beta|Y(1,x)+\Theta_{N}(x)|^{2},\log L)\mathbf{1}_{\{\|Y(1)+\Theta_{N}\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}\big\}\Big]dx

(2.24)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{M})+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta_{N}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt.

In view of the definitions of
\theta_{N}
(
2.20
),
Z_{N}
(
2.16
), and
f_{N}
(
2.11
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta_{N}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{1}\big\|-\frac{d}{dt}Z_{N}+\eta f_{N}\big\|_{H^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{2}dt

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\big\|\frac{d}{dt}Z_{N}\big\|_{H^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{2}dt+\eta^{2}\|f_{N}\|_{H^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{2}.

Using the bounds (
2.12
) and (
2.17
) of Lemmas
2.6
and
2.7
, we end up with

(2.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{1}\|\theta_{N}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}dt\lesssim N^{d+\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{\alpha}.

As for the first term in the right-hand side of (
2.24
), we first note that

\displaystyle|Y(1,x)+\Theta_{N}(x)|^{2}

\displaystyle=|Y(1,x)-Z_{N}(1,x)+\eta f_{N}(x)|^{2}\lesssim N^{d}+|Y(1,x)-Z_{N}(1,x)|^{2}

in view of (
2.14
). Thus provided that
L\gg N^{d}
we have for any
0<\delta\ll 1

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\{\min(\beta|Y(1,x)+\Theta_{N}(x)|^{2},\log L)\mathbf{1}_{\{\|Y(1)+\Theta_{N}\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}\big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\mathbb{E}\big\{\beta|Y(1,x)-Z_{N}(1,x)+\eta f_{N}(x)|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{\|Y(1)-Z_{N}(1)+\eta f_{N}\|_{L^{2}}\leq K,\|Y(1)-Z_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\log L\}}\big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\beta(1-\delta)\eta^{2}|f_{N}(x)|^{2}\mathbb{P}\big(\|Y(1)-Z_{N}(1)+\eta f_{N}\|_{L^{2}}\leq K,\|Y(1)-Z_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\log L\big)

(2.26)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-C(\delta)\mathbb{E}|Y(1,x)-Z_{N}(1,x)|^{2},

for some constant
C(\delta)>0
.

Assume that for any
K>0
, there exists
\eta=\eta(K)>0
such that

(2.27)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\|Y(1)-Z_{N}(1)+\eta f_{N}\|_{L^{2}}\leq K,\|Y(1)-Z_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\log L\big)\geq\frac{1}{2},

uniformly in in
L\gg N^{d}\gg 1
.

Then we can put (
2.25
)-(
2.26
)-(
2.27
) into (
2.24
) to get (recall
\gamma<0
)

(
2.22
)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\int_{\mathcal{M}}\exp\Big[\frac{\beta(1-\delta)\eta^{2}}{2}|f_{N}(x)|^{2}-C(\delta)\mathbb{E}|Y(1,x)-Z_{N}(1,x)|^{2})\Big]dx+O(N^{d+\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{\alpha})

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\int_{\{d(x,x_{0})\leq c_{\mathcal{M}}N^{-1}\}}\exp\Big[\frac{\beta(1-\delta)\eta^{2}}{2}|f_{N}(x)|^{2}-C(\delta)\mathbb{E}|Y(1,x)-Z_{N}(1,x)|^{2})\Big]dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+O(N^{d+\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{\alpha})

(2.28)

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma N^{-d}\exp\Big[\frac{\beta(1-\delta)\eta^{2}}{4}N^{\frac{d}{2}}-o(1))\Big]dx+O(N^{d+\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{\alpha}),

where
x_{0}
is as in Lemma
2.6
, and the last estimate comes from (
2.15
) in Lemma
2.6
and (
2.18
) in Lemma
2.7
.

Combining (
2.4
)-(
2.22
)-(
2.28
), we infer that there are constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>0
such that

\displaystyle-\log\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\alpha}}\Big[\exp\Big(\min\big(V_{\beta}(u),L\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}\Big)\Big]\leq\gamma C_{1}N^{-d}e^{C_{2}N^{\frac{d}{2}}}+C_{3}(N^{d+\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{\alpha}),

for any
L\gg N^{d}\gg 1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\alpha}}\Big[-\gamma\min(V_{\beta}(u),L)\mathbf{1}_{\{\|u\|_{L^{2}}\leq K\}}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\exp\Big(-\gamma C_{1}N^{-d}e^{C_{2}N^{\frac{d}{2}}}-C_{3}(N^{d+\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{\alpha})\Big)\to+\infty

as
N\to+\infty
since
\gamma<0
. This finally proves (
2.9
) by assuming (
2.27
).

To conclude, we prove that (
2.27
) holds for any
K>0
and
L\gg N^{d}\gg 1
, provided that
\eta(K)
is large enough. Indeed, it holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\big(\|Y(1)-Z_{N}(1)+\eta f_{N}\|_{L^{2}}>K\text{ or }\|Y(1)-Z_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}}\gg\log L\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\mathbb{P}\big(\|Y(1)-Z_{N}(1)+\eta f_{N}\|_{L^{2}}>K)+\mathbb{P}\big(\|Y(1)-Z_{N}(1)\|_{L^{\infty}}\gg\log L\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq K^{-2}\mathbb{E}\|Y(1)-Z_{N}(1)+\eta f_{N}\|_{L^{2}}+C(\log L)^{-p}\mathbb{E}\|Y(1)-Z_{N}(1)\|_{L^{\infty}}^{p}

by using Chebychev’s inequality with some large
p\gg 1
. Using then (
2.17
)-(
2.19
) in Lemma
2.7
and (
2.12
) in Lemma
2.6
, we get the bound

\displaystyle\qquad\leq CK^{-2}\big(N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{d-\alpha}+c_{\mathcal{M}}\eta\big)+(\log L)^{-p}p^{\frac{p}{2}}(N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{d+2\varepsilon-\alpha})^{\frac{p}{2}},

for any
p\gg 1
(independent of
L,N
) and
0<\varepsilon<\frac{\alpha-d}{2}
, and some constant
C>0
. Since
L\gg N^{d}\gg 1
, taking
\eta\ll K^{2}
ensures that

\displaystyle CK^{-2}\big(N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{d-\alpha}+c_{\mathcal{M}}\eta\big)+(\log L)^{-p}p^{\frac{p}{2}}(N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{d+2\varepsilon-\alpha})^{\frac{p}{2}}<\frac{1}{2}.

This finally proves (
2.27
).

∎

2.5.
Some technical lemmas

In this subsection, we present the proofs of
Lemmas
2.6
and
2.7
.

We start with the approximation lemma (Lemma
2.7
).

Proof of Lemma
2.7
.

For
\lambda_{n}\leq N
, let

(2.29)

\displaystyle X_{n}(t)=\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}B_{n}(t)-Z_{N,n}(t),

Then, from (
2.16
),
we see that
X_{n}(t)
satisfies
the following stochastic differential equation:

(2.30)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}dX_{n}(t)=-\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}X_{n}(t)dt+\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}dB_{n}(t)\\
X_{n}(0)=0\end{cases}

for
\lambda_{n}\leq N
.
The solution to this stochastic differential equation is thus given by

(2.31)

\displaystyle X_{n}(t)=\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}(t-s)}dB_{n}(s).

for
\lambda_{n}\leq N
.

To show (
2.17
), observe that
\frac{d}{dt}Z_{N}(t)=N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\langle\nabla\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}X_{N}
due to (
2.16
) and (
2.29
), where

\displaystyle X_{N}(t)=\Pi_{\leq N}Y(t)-Z_{N}(t)=\sum_{\lambda_{n}\leq N}X_{n}(t)\varphi_{n}.

Hence, from (
2.30
), the independence of
\{B_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
, and Ito’s isometry with (
2.31
), it holds for any
s\in\mathbb{R}

(2.32)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\Big\|\frac{d}{dt}Z_{N}(t)\Big\|_{H^{s}}^{2}dt

\displaystyle=N^{\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\big\|X_{N}(t)\big\|_{H^{s-\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{2}dt

(2.33)

\displaystyle\leq N^{\alpha}\sum_{\lambda_{n}\leq N}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{2s-\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{t}e^{-2\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}(t-t^{\prime})}dt^{\prime}dt

(2.34)

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{\alpha}\sum_{\lambda_{n}\leq N}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{2s-\alpha}N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}

(2.35)

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\max\big(1,N^{d+2s-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\big),

for any
N\gg 1
, where the last estimate follows from Weyl’s law (
1.15
). This is enough for (
2.17
).

For (
2.18
), we estimate similarly as above

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big|Y(1,x)-Z_{N}(1,x)\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}|X_{N}(1,x)|^{2}+\mathbb{E}|(1-\Pi_{\leq N}Y)(1,x)|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\lambda_{n}\leq N}|\varphi_{n}(x)|^{2}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-\alpha}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-2\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}N^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}(1-t)}dt+\sum_{\lambda_{n}>N}|\varphi_{n}(x)|^{2}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-\alpha}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\sum_{m\leq N}\sum_{\lambda_{n}\in(m,m+1]}\frac{|\varphi_{n}(x)|^{2}}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}+\sum_{m>N}\sum_{\lambda_{n}\in(m,m+1]}\frac{|\varphi_{n}(x)|^{2}}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\alpha}}

Applying then Corollary
2.2
, we can continue with

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\sum_{m\leq N}\sum_{\lambda_{n}\in(m,m+1]}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+\sum_{m>N}\sum_{\lambda_{n}\in(m,m+1]}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{-\alpha}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\max\big(1,N^{d-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\big)+N^{d-\alpha},

where the last estimate follows again from Weyl’s law (
1.15
). This proves (
2.18
).

Finally, to prove (
2.19
), we use a standard combination of Sobolev and Minkowski inequalities along with Khinchin’s inequality (Lemma
2.3
): thus for any
0<\varepsilon<\frac{\alpha-d}{2}
and
p\geq p_{\varepsilon}\gg 1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big\|Y(1)-Z_{N}(1)\big\|_{L^{\infty}}^{p}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\big\|Y(1)-Z_{N}(1)\big\|_{W^{\varepsilon,p_{\varepsilon}}}^{p}\lesssim\int_{\mathcal{M}}\mathbb{E}\big|\langle\nabla\rangle^{\varepsilon}(Y(1)-Z_{N}(1))\big|^{p}dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}}p^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big|\langle\nabla\rangle^{\varepsilon}(Y(1,x)-Z_{N}(1,x))\big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{p}{2}}

A computation similar to the one made above for (
2.18
) finally yields

\displaystyle\lesssim p^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big(N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\sum_{\lambda_{n}\leq N}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{2\varepsilon-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+\sum_{\lambda_{n}>N}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{2\varepsilon-\alpha}\Big)^{\frac{p}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim p^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big(N^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}+N^{d+2\varepsilon-\alpha}\Big)^{\frac{p}{2}}.

This proves (
2.19
).

∎

To conclude this subsection, we present the proof of Lemma
2.6
.

Proof of Lemma
2.6
.

Recall that
f_{N}
is given by (
2.11
). In particular a straightforward computation using Corollary
2.2
and Weyl’s law (
1.15
) yields

\displaystyle\|f_{N}\|_{H^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}^{2}=N^{-d}\sum_{\lambda\in(N,2N]}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\alpha}|\varphi_{n}(x_{0})|^{2}\lesssim N^{-d}\sum_{\lambda_{n}\sim N}\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\alpha}\lesssim N^{\alpha},

and, using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and repeating the previous computation,

\displaystyle\|f_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}}N^{-\frac{d}{2}}\sum_{\lambda\in(N,2N]}|\varphi_{n}(x)\varphi_{n}(x_{0})|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}}N^{-\frac{d}{2}}\Big(\sum_{\lambda_{n}\in(N,2N]}|\varphi_{n}(x)|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\sum_{\lambda_{n}\in(N,2N]}|\varphi_{n}(x_{0})|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{\frac{d}{2}}.

This proves (
2.13
) and (
2.14
).

For (
2.12
), we observe that

\displaystyle\|f_{N}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=N^{-d}\sum_{\lambda_{n}\in(N,2N]}|\varphi_{n}(x_{0})|^{2}=N^{-d}\big(e(x_{0},x_{0},(2N)^{2})-e(x_{0},x_{0},N^{2})\big)

where
e(x,y,\Lambda^{2})
is the spectral function of
\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}}
, defined in (
2.1
) above. In view of Lemma
2.1
, we can thus continue with

\displaystyle=N^{-d}\big(c_{d}(2N)^{d}-c_{d}N^{d}+O(N^{d-1})\big)=(2^{d}-1)c_{d}+O(N^{-1}).

This proves (
2.12
).

To show the remaining bound (
2.15
), observe as above that

(2.36)

\displaystyle f_{N}(x)=N^{-\frac{d}{2}}\big(e(x,x_{0},(2N)^{2})-e(x,x_{0},N^{2})\big).

In particular with Lemma
2.1
this implies

(2.37)

\displaystyle f_{N}(x_{0})=N^{-\frac{d}{2}}\big(c_{d}(2N)^{d}-c_{d}N^{d}+O(N^{d-1})\big)=(2^{d}-1)c_{d}N^{\frac{d}{2}}+O(N^{\frac{d}{2}-1}).

In order to control the difference
f_{N}(x)-f_{N}(x_{0})
, we recall the following refined version of Lemma
2.1
proved in
[
35
, Theorem 4.4]
.

Lemma 2.8
.

There are some constants
r,R,C>0
such that for any
x\in B_{r}(x_{0})=\{d(x,x_{0})<r\}
, it holds

(2.38)

\displaystyle\Big|e(x,x_{0},\Lambda^{2})-(2\pi)^{-d}\int_{\{|\xi|\leq\Lambda\}}e^{i\psi(\kappa(x),\xi)}d\xi\Big|\leq C\langle\Lambda\rangle^{d-1},

where
\kappa:B_{r}(x_{0})\to B_{R}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
are normal coordinates centred at
x_{0}
, and the phase
\psi\in C^{\infty}(B_{R}(0)\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies

(2.39)

\displaystyle\psi(z,\xi)

\displaystyle=z\cdot\xi+O(|z|^{2}|\xi|)\text{ as }z\to 0.

Thus, putting (
2.38
) into (
2.36
) and using the mean value inequality and the property (
2.39
) of
\psi
, we get for any
d(x,x_{0})\ll N^{-1}
:

\displaystyle\big|f_{N}(x)-f_{N}(x_{0})\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-\frac{d}{2}}\Big|\int_{\{N<|\xi|\leq 2N\}}\big[e^{i\psi(\kappa(x),\xi)}-1\big]d\xi\Big|+O(N^{\frac{d}{2}-1})

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{\{|\xi|\sim N\}}|\psi(\kappa(x),\xi)|d\xi+O(N^{\frac{d}{2}-1})

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-\frac{d}{2}}d(x,x_{0})\int_{\{|\xi|\sim N\}}|\xi|d\xi+O(N^{\frac{d}{2}-1})

(2.40)

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{\frac{d}{2}+1}d(x,x_{0})+O(N^{\frac{d}{2}-1})\ll N^{\frac{d}{2}}.

Thus (
2.15
) follows from (
2.37
) and (
2.5
).

∎

3.
Weak invariance of the Gibbs measure for lower dispersion

In this section, we give the proof of Theorem
1.3
(i). We mainly follow
[
17
,
54
]
. In this section we thus fix
\gamma>0
,
\alpha>d
and
0<s<\frac{\alpha-d}{2}
.

3.1.
Preliminary estimates

We start by establishing the following moment bound for the potential
V_{\beta}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\beta>0
. Then the following hold:

(i)
If
0<\beta<\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}
and
1\leq p<(\beta\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}})^{-1}
, then
V_{\beta}\in L^{p}(\mu_{\alpha})
. In particular
V_{\beta}(\Pi_{\leq N}u)\to V_{\beta}(u)
in
L^{p}(\mu_{\alpha})
as
N\to\infty
.

(ii)
If
\beta>\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}
then
V_{\beta}\not\in L^{1}(\mu_{\alpha})
.

Proof.

We start by proving (i). We thus assume that
0<\beta<\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}
and fix
1\leq p<(\beta\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}})^{-1}
. In view of the almost sure convergence of
V_{\beta}(\Pi_{\leq N}u)
(Lemma
2.4
) and Fatou’s lemma, it suffices to show that

(3.1)

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\alpha}}\big[V_{\beta}(\Pi_{\leq N}u)^{p}\big]<+\infty.

Recall that
\Pi_{\leq N}
is the (sharp) spectral projector onto the frequencies
\{\lambda_{n}\leq N\}
. For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we then write

(3.2)

\displaystyle d_{N}=\#\Lambda_{N}=\#\{n,~\lambda_{n}\leq N\},

and using that
\mu_{\alpha}
is the law of
u_{\alpha}
in (
1.10
), we estimate using Jensen’s inequality in
x
with the compactness of
\mathcal{M}
and Fubini-Tonelli’s theorem:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\alpha}}\big[V_{\beta}(\Pi_{\leq N}u)^{p}\big]

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\Big[\int_{\mathcal{M}}\exp\Big(\beta\big|\sum_{\lambda_{n}\leq N}\frac{g_{n}}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\varphi_{n}(x)\big|^{2}\Big)dx\Big]^{p}d\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathcal{M}}\int_{\Omega}\exp\Big(p\beta\big|\sum_{\lambda_{n}\leq N}\frac{g_{n}}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\varphi_{n}(x)\big|^{2}\Big)d\mathbb{P}dx

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathcal{M}}\int_{\Omega}\exp\Big(p\beta\sum_{n,m\in\Lambda_{N}}\frac{g_{n}\overline{g}_{m}}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\langle\lambda_{m}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\varphi_{n}(x)\varphi_{m}(x)\Big)d\mathbb{P}dx

Recalling that
g_{n}
are iid standard complex Gaussian random variables,
g_{n}=\frac{h_{n}+i\ell_{n}}{\sqrt{2}}
with
h_{n},\ell_{n}
iid standard real-valued Gaussian random variables, we can continue with

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathcal{M}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d_{N}}}\exp\Big(p\beta\sum_{n,m=1}^{d_{N}}\frac{y_{n}y_{m}+z_{n}z_{m}+i(z_{n}y_{m}-y_{n}z_{m})}{2\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\langle\lambda_{m}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\varphi_{n}(x)\varphi_{m}(x)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{n=1}^{d_{N}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}y_{n}^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}z_{n}^{2}}\frac{dy_{n}dz_{n}}{2\pi}dx

(3.3)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathcal{M}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{N}}}\exp\Big(p\beta\sum_{n,m=1}^{d_{N}}\frac{y_{n}y_{m}}{2\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\langle\lambda_{m}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\varphi_{n}(x)\varphi_{m}(x)-\frac{y_{n}^{2}}{2}\Big)\prod_{n=1}^{d_{N}}\frac{dy_{n}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\bigg)^{2}dx,

where we used the symmetry in
n
and
m
. Defining the symmetric matrix

(3.4)

\displaystyle A(x)=\Big(\frac{\varphi_{n}(x)\varphi_{m}(x)}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\langle\lambda_{m}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\Big)_{n,m=1}^{d_{N}}\in\mathcal{S}_{d_{N}}(\mathbb{R}),

we can write the inner integral as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{N}}}\exp\Big(p\beta\sum_{n,m=1}^{d_{N}}\frac{y_{n}y_{m}}{2\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\langle\lambda_{m}\rangle^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\varphi_{n}(x)\varphi_{m}(x)-\frac{y_{n}^{2}}{2}\Big)\prod_{n=1}^{d_{N}}\frac{dy_{n}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}

(3.5)

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{N}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}y\cdot(I_{d_{N}}-p\beta A(x))y}\frac{dy}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d_{N}}{2}}}.

Then, since
\mathrm{rank}A(x)\leq 1
in view of (
3.4
), we have that the only non zero eigenvalue of
A(x)
is

\mathrm{tr}A(x)=\sum_{\lambda_{n}\leq N}\frac{|\varphi_{n}(x)|^{2}}{\langle\lambda_{n}\rangle^{\alpha}}=\sigma_{\alpha,N}(x).

Thus, since
A(x)
is symmetric, we find that the integral in (
3.5
) is finite if and only if
I_{d_{N}}-p\beta A(x)
is positive definite, which corresponds to

(3.6)

\displaystyle p\beta\sigma_{\alpha,N}(x)<1.

In that case,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{N}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}y\cdot(I_{d_{N}}-p\beta A(x))y}\frac{dy}{(2\pi)^{\frac{d_{N}}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{det}\big(I_{d_{N}}-p\beta A(x)\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}=(1-p\beta\sigma_{\alpha,N}(x))^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Since for any
x\in\mathcal{M}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
it holds
\sigma_{\alpha,N}(x)\leq\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}
, under the conditions
0<\beta<\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}
and
1\leq p<(\beta\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}})^{-1}
, we see that (
3.6
) holds uniformly in
x\in\mathcal{M}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, and putting (
3.3
)-(
3.5
)-(
3.7
) together and using the compactness of
\mathcal{M}
we get

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\alpha}}\big[V_{\beta}(\Pi_{\leq N}u)^{p}\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{1-p\beta\|\sigma_{\alpha,N}\|_{L^{\infty}}}=\frac{1}{1-p\beta\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}}<+\infty.

This proves (
3.1
), hence Lemma
3.1
(i). This even provides the slightly stronger bound

(3.8)

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\Big\|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\alpha}}\big[e^{p\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2}}\big]\Big\|_{L^{\infty}}<+\infty.

As for (ii) in the case
\beta>\|\sigma_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{-1}
, since
\sigma_{\alpha}\in C(\mathcal{M})
, there exists
x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
and
r>0
such that
\beta>\sigma_{\alpha,N}(x)
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in B_{r}(x_{0})
. Thus the computation above shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\alpha}}\big[V_{\beta}(\Pi_{\leq N}u)\big]\geq\mathrm{Vol}(B_{r}(x_{0}))\cdot\infty=+\infty,

for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
. This proves Lemma
3.1
(ii).
∎

In the proof of Theorem
1.3
(i), we will also make use of the following result, which is a straightforward adaptation of
[
17
, Lemma 3.3]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
T>0
and
1<p,q<\infty
,
\theta_{0}\in[0,1]
be such that
(1-\theta_{0})(\frac{1}{q}-1)+\frac{\theta_{0}}{p}=0
.
Suppose that

u\in L^{p}_{T}H^{s_{1}}
and
\partial_{t}u\in L^{q}_{T}H^{s_{2}}

for some
s_{2}\leq s_{1}
.
Then, for
\varepsilon>(1-\theta_{0})(s_{1}-s_{2})
, we have

(3.9)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s_{1}}}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s_{1}+\varepsilon}}^{\theta_{0}}\|u\|_{W^{1,q}_{T}H^{s_{2}}}^{1-\theta_{0}}.

Moreover, writing
\theta_{1}=\frac{s_{1}-s_{2}}{s_{1}-s_{2}+\varepsilon}\in[0,1]
, it holds for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in[-T,T]

(3.10)

\displaystyle\|u(t_{2})-u(t_{1})\|_{H^{s_{1}}}\lesssim|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{(1-\theta_{1})(1-\frac{1}{q})}\|u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s_{1}+\varepsilon}}^{\theta_{1}}\|u\|_{W^{1,q}_{T}H^{s_{2}}}^{1-\theta_{1}}.

Proof.

As in
[
17
, Lemmas 3.2 and 3.3]
, we perform a Littlewood-Paley decomposition

u=\sum_{M\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}}\psi_{M}(-\Delta)u=\sum_{M\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}}u_{M},

where
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
,
\supp\psi\subset[\frac{1}{4},4]
,
\psi\equiv 1
on
[-\frac{1}{2},2]
, and
\psi_{M}(-\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}})=\psi(-M^{-2}\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}})
is defined via the functional calculus:

\displaystyle u_{M}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\psi(M^{-2}\lambda_{n}^{2})\langle u,\varphi_{n}\rangle_{L^{2}}\varphi_{n}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s_{1}-\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{M\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}}L^{s_{1}-\varepsilon}\|u_{M}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}}=\sum_{M\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}}M^{s_{1}-\varepsilon}\sup_{\|v\|_{L^{2}}\leq 1}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\langle u_{M}(t),v\rangle_{L^{2}}.

Then we apply the Gagliardo-Nirenberg interpolation inequality (see for example Theorem 12.83 in
[
42
]
)

(3.11)

\displaystyle\|w\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}}\lesssim\|w\|_{L^{p}_{T}}^{\theta_{0}}\|w\|_{W^{1,q}_{T}}^{1-\theta_{0}},

which holds for any
1\leq p,q\leq\infty
and
\theta_{0}\in[0,1]
such that
(1-\theta_{0})(\frac{1}{q}-1)+\frac{\theta}{p}=0
, to the function
w(t)=\langle\psi_{M}(-\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}})u(t),v\rangle_{L^{2}}
. Thus

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\langle u_{L}(t),v\rangle_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big\|\langle u_{M}(t),v\rangle_{L^{2}}\big\|_{L^{p}_{T}}^{\theta_{0}}\big\|\langle u_{M}(t),v\rangle_{L^{2}}\big\|_{W^{1,q}_{T}}^{1-\theta_{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u_{M}\|_{L^{p}_{T}L^{2}}^{\theta_{0}}\|u_{M}\|_{W^{1,q}_{t}L^{2}}^{1-\theta_{0}}.

This yields

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{s_{1}-\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{M\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}}M^{s_{1}-\varepsilon}\|u_{M}\|_{L^{q}_{T}L^{2}}^{\theta_{0}}\|u_{M}\|_{W^{1,p}_{t}L^{2}}^{1-\theta_{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{M\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}}M^{s_{1}-\varepsilon-\theta_{0}s_{1}-(1-\theta_{0})s_{2}}\|u_{M}\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s_{1}}}^{\theta_{0}}\|u_{M}\|_{W^{1,q}_{t}H^{s_{2}}}^{1-\theta_{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{M\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}}M^{s_{1}-\varepsilon-\theta_{0}s_{1}-(1-\theta_{0})s_{2}}\|u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s_{1}}}^{\theta_{0}}\|u\|_{W^{1,q}_{t}H^{s_{2}}}^{1-\theta_{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{p}_{T}H^{s_{1}}}^{\theta_{0}}\|u\|_{W^{1,q}_{t}H^{s_{2}}}^{1-\theta_{0}}

since
\varepsilon>(1-\theta_{0})(s_{1}-s_{2})
. This proves (
3.9
). The proof of (
3.10
) follows similarly as in
[
17
, Lemma 3.3]
: combine the interpolation inequality

\displaystyle\|u(t_{1})-u(t_{2})\|_{H^{s_{1}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|u(t_{1})-u(t_{2})\|_{H^{s_{1}+\varepsilon}}^{\theta_{1}}\|u(t_{1})-u(t_{2})\|_{H^{s_{2}}}^{1-\theta_{1}}

with the bound

\displaystyle\|u(t_{1})-u(t_{2})\|_{H^{s_{2}}}=\Big\|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\partial_{t}udt\Big\|_{H^{s_{2}}}\lesssim|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{1-\frac{1}{q}}\|u\|_{W^{1,q}_{T}H^{s_{2}}}

Combining the two previous estimates finally proves (
3.10
).
∎

3.2.
From the Gibbs measure to measures on paths

The general strategy of
[
17
,
54
]
to get weak invariance of the Gibbs measure is to look for stationary solutions with law
\rho_{\alpha,\beta}
to (
1.1
) as limits of solutions to (
1.18
) having law
\rho_{\alpha,\beta,N}
. Thus we start by proving global well-posedness for the truncated equation (
1.18
)

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}u_{N}+(-\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u_{N}=2\gamma\beta\Pi_{\leq N}\Big[e^{\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2}}\Pi_{\leq N}u\Big].

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then,
the truncated exponential NLS (
1.18
)
is globally well-posed in
H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
.
Moreover, the truncated Gibbs measure
\rho_{\alpha,\beta,N}

is invariant under the dynamics of
(
1.18
).

Proof.

We write (
1.18
) under the Duhamel formulation

(3.12)

\displaystyle u_{N}(t)=e^{it(-\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}u_{N}(0)-2i\gamma\beta\int_{0}^{t}e^{i(t-t^{\prime})(-\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}\Pi_{\leq N}\Big[e^{\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2}}\Pi_{\leq N}u\Big](t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}.

Write
\Gamma_{N}(u_{N})
for the right-hand side of (
3.12
). Then for
T>0
and
u\in C([-T,T];L^{2}(\mathcal{M}))
, using the compactness of
\mathcal{M}
, it holds

\displaystyle\big\|\Gamma_{N}(u)\big\|_{C_{T}L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|u(0)\|_{L^{2}}+\Big\|\Pi_{\leq N}\Big[e^{\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2}}\Pi_{\leq N}u\Big]\Big\|_{L^{1}_{T}L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|u(0)\|_{H^{s}}+CT\Big\|\Pi_{\leq N}\Big[e^{\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2}}\Pi_{\leq N}u\Big]\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|u(0)\|_{H^{s}}+CTe^{\beta\|\Pi_{\leq N}u\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{\infty}}^{2}}\big\|\Pi_{\leq N}u\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|u(0)\|_{H^{s}}+CTe^{\beta N^{d}\|u\|_{C_{T}L^{2}}^{2}}\|u\|_{C_{T}L^{2}}.

Here we used the Bernstein type inequality

\|\Pi_{\leq N}u\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim N^{\frac{d}{2}}\|u\|_{L^{2}},

which follows from writing
{\displaystyle\Pi_{\leq N}u=\int_{\mathcal{M}}e(x,y,N^{2})u(y)dy}
with
e(x,y,\Lambda)
the spectral function (
2.38
), and from the estimate

\displaystyle\|e(x,y,N^{2})\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}L^{2}_{y}}=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}}\Big(\sum_{\lambda_{n}\leq N}|\varphi_{n}(x)|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\sup_{x\in\mathcal{M}}e(x,x,N^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim N^{\frac{d}{2}}

which is a consequence of Lemma
2.1
.

Thus for
T=T_{N}(R)\sim e^{-\beta N^{d}R^{2}}
,
\Gamma_{N}
maps the ball
B_{R,T}
of radius
R=2\|u_{0}\|
of
C([-T,T];L^{2}(\mathcal{M}))
in itself. Similarly, using the mean value theorem, it holds for any
u_{1},u_{2}\in B_{R,T}

\displaystyle\big\|\Gamma_{N}(u_{1})-\Gamma_{N}(u_{2})\big\|_{C_{T}L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|u_{1}(0)-u_{2}(0)\|_{L^{2}}+CT\Big\|\Pi_{\leq N}\Big[e^{\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u_{1}|^{2}}\Pi_{\leq N}u_{1}\Big]-\Pi_{\leq N}\Big[e^{\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u_{2}|^{2}}\Pi_{\leq N}u_{2}\Big]\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|u_{1}(0)-u_{2}(0)\|_{L^{2}}+CT\Big\|e^{\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u_{1}|^{2}}\Pi_{\leq N}(u_{1}-u_{2})\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+CT\Big\|\big|\Pi_{\leq N}(u_{1}-u_{2})\big|\big(|\Pi_{\leq N}u_{1}|+|\Pi_{\leq N}u_{2}|\big)\big(e^{\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u_{1}|^{2}}+e^{\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u_{2}|^{2}}\big)\Pi_{\leq N}u_{2}\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|u_{1}(0)-u_{2}(0)\|_{L^{2}}+CT\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{C_{T}L^{2}}e^{\beta CN^{d}\|u_{1}\|_{C_{T}L^{2}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+CTN^{2d}\|u_{1}-u_{2}\|_{C_{T}L^{2}}\big(\|u_{1}\|_{C_{T}L^{2}}+\|u_{2}\|_{C_{T}L^{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\big(e^{\beta CN^{d}\|u_{1}\|_{C_{T}L^{2}}^{2}}+e^{\beta CN^{d}\|u_{2}\|_{C_{T}L^{2}}^{2}}\big)\|u_{2}\|_{C_{T}L^{2}}.

This shows that for
T_{N}\sim N^{-2d}R^{-2}e^{-\beta CN^{d}R^{2}}
,
\Gamma_{N}
is also a contraction on
B_{R,T_{N}}
. Thus by Banach fixed point theorem, there is a solution
u_{N}
to (
3.12
), unique in
B_{R,T_{N}}
. Standard iteration argument shows that uniqueness holds in the whole of
C([-T_{N},T_{N}];L^{2}(\mathcal{M}))
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\|u_{N}(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\langle u_{N}(t),\partial_{t}u_{N}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=2\Re\Big\{\int_{\mathcal{M}}\overline{u}_{N}\cdot i\Big((-\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u_{N}(t)-2\gamma\beta\Pi_{\leq N}\big[e^{\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2}}\Pi_{\leq N}u\big]\Big)\Big\}dx

\displaystyle=2\int_{\mathcal{M}}\Re i\Big\{\big|(-\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}u_{N}(t)\big|^{2}-2\gamma\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2}e^{\beta|\Pi_{\leq N}u|^{2}}\Big\}dx=0,

after integrating by parts. The conservation of the
L^{2}
norm of
u_{N}
thus shows that (
1.18
) is actually globally well-posed in
C(\mathbb{R};L^{2}(\mathcal{M}))
. At last, since
\Pi_{\leq N}^{2}=\Pi_{\leq N}
, in view of the equation (
1.18
) it holds

u_{N}=\Pi_{\leq N}u_{N}+(1-\Pi_{\leq N})e^{it(-\Delta_{\mathbf{\mathrm{g}}})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}}u_{N}(0),

which shows that actually
u_{N}\in C(\mathbb{R};H^{s}(\mathcal{M}))
if
u_{N}(0)\in H^{s}(\mathcal{M})
